Conditional on this overlap, the normalized sum and difference are again [(eq:orientation-pair-moment)](#eq:orientation-pair-moment) the orthogonal pair above. Independence of the two spherical points consequently gives the exact identity $$\int_{-1}^1 M(q)f_S(q)\,dq=S(1)^2.
 \label{eq:orientation-spherical-second-moment}$$

The moment identities reduce concentration to a comparison of the two overlap laws. Because $\mathbb E_O I=S(1)$, it is enough to prove $$\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q_n}p_I(q)M(q)
 \le (1+o(1))\int_{-1}^1 M(q)f_S(q)\,dq$$ with a deterministic $o(1)$ uniform over $\mathcal G_n$. We will use a variational estimate for large overlaps and a direct comparison of the unweighted overlap laws near zero.

<a id="eq:orientation-pair-bound"></a>
First consider the size of the pair integral. By evenness it suffices to take $q\ge0$. Conditional on $U$ in <a href="#eq:orientation-pair-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-pair-moment">[eq:orientation-pair-moment]</a>, the vector $V$ is uniform on the radius-$\sqrt n$ sphere in $U^\perp$. The codimension-one bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:partition-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:partition-bounds">11</a> controls its integral at parameter $1-q$, uniformly in $U$. The full-space bound then controls the remaining integral at parameter $1+q$. Dividing by the lower bound for $S(1)^2$ from the same lemma gives $$\frac{M(q)}{S(1)^2}
 \le Cn^2\exp\!\left(n\bigl[B(1+|q|)+B(1-|q|)-2B(1)\bigr]\right).
 \label{eq:orientation-pair-bound}$$ This remains valid at $|q|=1$, since the integral with parameter zero equals one.

The cube overlap mass satisfies $p_I(q)\le e^{-nq^2/2}$. Indeed, for a mean of $n$ independent uniform signs, the exponential moment bound $\cosh t\le e^{t^2/2}$ follows by integrating $(\log\cosh t)''\le1$ twice from zero. Optimizing the resulting Chernoff bound and using symmetry gives the stated bound on each lattice mass. Thus a margin below $q^2/2$ in the exponent of <a href="#eq:orientation-pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-pair-bound">[eq:orientation-pair-bound]</a> will suppress large overlaps.

We split the overlap range at $$q_0=n^{-7/24}.$$ The proof uses three scale relations: $$nq_0^3=n^{1/8}\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad
 nq_0^4=n^{-1/6}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \frac{s_0}{q_0^2}=L_n n^{-1/12}\longrightarrow0.$$ The first turns a cubic variational margin into a negligible tail. The second permits a relative local comparison of the unweighted cube and sphere overlap laws. The third absorbs the spectral error in the cubic estimate; it uses $L_n\le\log n$.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The variational deficit

The next lemma supplies the margin needed in <a href="#eq:orientation-pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-pair-bound">[eq:orientation-pair-bound]</a> for $|q|\ge q_0$.

<a id="stmt-lem-cubic-deficit"></a>
<div id="lem:cubic-deficit" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cubic-deficit"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Cubic deficit). *There is a universal constant $c>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly for spectra in $\mathcal G_n$ and $q_0\le q\le1$, $$B(1+q)+B(1-q)-2B(1)
 \le \frac{q^2}{2}-cq^3.
 \label{eq:cubic-deficit}$$ One may take $c=1/24$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cubic-deficit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cubic-deficit</summary>

<a id="eq:orientation-saddle-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:orientation-supercritical-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* We estimate the derivative of the spherical variational value on both sides of the critical parameter $a=1$. Close to that parameter the resolvent estimates give a quantitative margin; away from it a bounded-convergence argument suffices. The saddle equation in Lemma <a href="#lem:spherical-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spherical-identity">9</a> and implicit differentiation show that $z_a$ is continuously differentiable in $a$. Differentiating the objective at its minimizer gives $$B'(a)=\frac1{2n}\sum_{i=1}^n
       \frac{\lambda_i}{z_a-a\lambda_i},
 \qquad
 B'(a)=\frac{z_a-1}{2a}\quad(a>0).
 \label{eq:orientation-saddle-derivative}$$ The first identity also holds at $a=0$, with a one-sided derivative at the endpoint. Since the positive weights $(z_a-a\lambda_i)^{-1}$ sum to $n$, $$|B'(a)|\le\frac12\max_i|\lambda_i|\le\frac32
 \qquad(0\le a\le2).$$ For $a>0$, put $r_a=z_a/a$ and $$m_n(s)=\frac1n\sum_i(2+s-\lambda_i)^{-1}.$$ The saddle equation is equivalently $$\frac1n\sum_i(r_a-\lambda_i)^{-1}=a.$$ At $s=s_0$, the spectral estimates give $m_n(s_0)=1-\sqrt{s_0}+o(\sqrt{s_0})<1$. Thus, for $a=1+u$ with $0\le u\le1$, monotonicity of this resolvent gives $r_a<2+s_0$ and hence $$B'(1+u)
 \le \frac{1+u}{2}-\frac{u^2}{2(1+u)}+\frac{s_0}{2}.
 \label{eq:orientation-supercritical-derivative}$$

The upper derivative estimate must be paired with a lower estimate below $a=1$. The small-overlap range requires the following quantitative comparison. If $4\sqrt{s_0}\le u\le1/4$, set $t=u^2/(1-u)$. Then $t\le1/12$ and $0.9t\ge14.4s_0$. The function $R$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:spectral">5</a> satisfies $$R(t)=1-u,\qquad
 -R'(s)=\frac{2+s}{2\sqrt{s(4+s)}}-\frac12
        \ge \frac{c_1}{\sqrt{s}}
        \quad(0<s\le1/12)$$ for a universal $c_1>0$. Integrating the last bound from $0.9t$ to $t$ therefore gives $$R(0.9t)-(1-u)\ge c_2\sqrt{t}\ge c_2u.$$ The error in $m_n(0.9t)=R(0.9t)+o(\sqrt{t})$ is uniform over the indicated range. It is smaller than this fixed positive margin for all sufficiently large $n$. Consequently $m_n(0.9t)>1-u$, so monotonicity implies $r_{1-u}\ge2+0.9t$. Substitution in <a href="#eq:orientation-saddle-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-saddle-derivative">[eq:orientation-saddle-derivative]</a> yields <a id="eq:orientation-subcritical-derivative"></a>$$B'(1-u)\ge\frac{1-u}{2}-\frac{u^2}{20(1-u)}
 \qquad(4\sqrt{s_0}\le u\le1/4).
 \label{eq:orientation-subcritical-derivative}$$ For $0\le u\le4\sqrt{s_0}$, use instead $r_{1-u}>\lambda_1=2+o(s_0)$ to obtain $$B'(1-u)
 \ge\frac{1-u}{2}-\frac{u^2}{2(1-u)}-o(s_0)
 \ge\frac{1-u}{2}-Cs_0.$$

Write $F(q)=B(1+q)+B(1-q)-2B(1)$. On $0\le u\le1/4$, $$\frac1{2(1+u)}-\frac1{20(1-u)}\ge\frac13.$$ Combining the preceding bounds, and absorbing $u^2\le16s_0$ on the short interval $u\le4\sqrt{s_0}$ into the error, gives $$F'(u)\le u-\frac{u^2}{3}+Cs_0
 \qquad(0\le u\le1/4).$$ Since $F(0)=0$, <a id="eq:orientation-small-overlap-deficit"></a>$$F(q)\le\frac{q^2}{2}-\frac{q^3}{9}+Cs_0q
 \qquad(0\le q\le1/4).
 \label{eq:orientation-small-overlap-deficit}$$ For $q\ge q_0$, the last error divided by $q^3$ is at most $$C\frac{s_0}{q_0^2}=CL_n n^{-1/12}\longrightarrow0.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:cubic-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-deficit">[eq:cubic-deficit]</a> holds on $[q_0,1/4]$, even with $c=1/18$.

It remains to cover overlaps bounded away from zero. Here a uniform additive error tending to zero is enough. Fix $a\in(0,1)$. The identity $R(a+a^{-1}-2)=a$, the strict monotonicity of $R$, and the spectral resolvent estimate imply $$r_a\longrightarrow a+a^{-1},
 \qquad B'(a)\longrightarrow a/2.$$ Indeed, evaluating the empirical resolvent at $a+a^{-1}\pm\delta$, with $\delta>0$ small enough that the lower point is above $2$, traps $r_a$ between these two points for all large $n$. The upper resolvent parameter needed here is finite for each fixed $a$. No estimate uniform as $a\downarrow0$ is used.

The bound $|B'|\le3/2$ now gives, by dominated convergence, $$\rho_n:=\int_0^1|B'(a)-a/2|\,da\longrightarrow0.$$ Integrating <a href="#eq:orientation-supercritical-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-supercritical-derivative">[eq:orientation-supercritical-derivative]</a> and bounding the subcritical error by $\rho_n$ gives, uniformly for $0\le q\le1$, <a id="eq:orientation-macroscopic-deficit"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 F(q)
 &\le \frac{q^2}{2}
       -\int_0^q\frac{u^2}{2(1+u)}\,du+\rho_n+\frac{s_0}{2} \notag\\
 &\le \frac{q^2}{2}-\frac{q^3}{12}+\rho_n+\frac{s_0}{2}.
 \label{eq:orientation-macroscopic-deficit}
\end{aligned}$$ When $q\ge1/4$, the cubic term is bounded away from zero, so the last two errors are absorbed with $c=1/24$. This also covers $q=1$.

The conclusion must hold uniformly over the entire deterministic spectral event. The estimates in the shrinking range already have that uniformity. The same is true of $\rho_n\to0$: otherwise one could select spectra from $\mathcal G_n$ along a violating subsequence. Their deterministic spectral bounds would still imply the pointwise convergence and bounded domination just proved, a contradiction. Thus the two ranges give the claimed uniform statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Concentration over the orientation

<a id="stmt-prop-orientation-concentration"></a>
<div id="prop:orientation-concentration" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (Conditional partition concentration). *For spectra in $\mathcal G_n$, $$\mathbb E_O I=S(1),
 \qquad
 \sup_{\Lambda\in\mathcal G_n}
 \mathbb E_O\!\left[\left(\frac{I}{S(1)}-1\right)^2\right]\longrightarrow0.$$ In particular, under the GOE law, $I/S(1)\to1$ in probability.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-orientation-concentration"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-orientation-concentration</summary>

*Proof.* The first-moment identity was established above. We now bound the exact second moment by treating the two overlap regions separately.

<a id="eq:orientation-tail"></a>
*Large overlaps.* Combining <a href="#eq:orientation-pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-pair-bound">[eq:orientation-pair-bound]</a>, the Chernoff bound $p_I(q)\le e^{-nq^2/2}$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:cubic-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cubic-deficit">15</a> gives $$p_I(q)\frac{M(q)}{S(1)^2}
 \le Cn^2 e^{-cn|q|^3}
 \qquad(q\in\mathcal Q_n,\ |q|\ge q_0).$$ There are at most $n+1$ lattice points. Since $nq_0^3=n^{1/8}$, $$\sum_{\substack{q\in\mathcal Q_n\\ |q|\ge q_0}}
       p_I(q)\frac{M(q)}{S(1)^2}
 \le Cn^3e^{-c n^{1/8}}=: \tau_n.
 \label{eq:orientation-tail}$$ In particular, $\tau_n$ is smaller than every fixed negative power of $n$.

<a id="eq:orientation-local-density"></a>
*Central overlaps.* The remaining obstacle is that $M(q)$ may vary too rapidly for a derivative-based Riemann-sum estimate. We compare the unweighted overlap laws locally, then move each lattice mass outward, where $M$ is larger. Let $\Delta=2/n$. Stirling’s formula, uniformly for $|q|<q_0$, gives $$\begin{gathered}
 p_I(q)=
 \sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi n(1-q^2)}}\,
 e^{-nJ(q)}\bigl(1+O(n^{-1})\bigr),\\
 J(q)=\frac{(1+q)\log(1+q)+(1-q)\log(1-q)}2.
\end{gathered}$$ Here $J(q)=q^2/2+O(q^4)$. Likewise $c_n=\sqrt{n/(2\pi)}(1+O(n^{-1}))$ and $\log(1-q^2)=-q^2+O(q^4)$. Dividing the two expressions shows that $$\frac{p_I(q)}{\Delta f_S(q)}
 =\exp\!\bigl(O(n^{-1}+q^2+nq^4)\bigr)=1+o(1),
 \qquad |q|<q_0,
 \label{eq:orientation-local-density}$$ with a uniform relative error, since $nq_0^4\to0$.

Assign to each retained lattice point the outward bin <a id="eq:orientation-bins"></a>$$\mathcal B_q=
 \begin{cases}
 [q,q+\Delta],&q>0,\\
 [q-\Delta,q],&q<0,\\
 [-\Delta/2,\Delta/2],&q=0.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:orientation-bins}$$ For even $n$, the lattice near zero is $\ldots,-2\Delta,-\Delta,0,\Delta,2\Delta,\ldots$. The centered zero bin leaves gaps between $\Delta/2$ and $\Delta$, and between $-\Delta$ and $-\Delta/2$. For odd $n$, the nearest points are $-\Delta/2,\Delta/2$, whose outward bins leave the gap $(-\Delta/2,\Delta/2)$. In both cases all bin interiors are disjoint. Every $r\in\mathcal B_q$ satisfies $|r|\ge|q|$, and all these bins lie in $[-q_0-\Delta,q_0+\Delta]\subset(-1,1)$ for large $n$.

<a id="eq:orientation-bin-comparison"></a>
On each such bin, $$\left|\log\frac{f_S(r)}{f_S(q)}\right|
 \le Cn(q_0+\Delta)\Delta\le C(q_0+\Delta),$$ because $(\log f_S)'(r)=-(n-3)r/(1-r^2)$. It follows from <a href="#eq:orientation-local-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-local-density">[eq:orientation-local-density]</a> that there is a deterministic $\delta_n\to0$ such that, for every nonnegative even function $h$ nondecreasing in $|q|$, $$p_I(q)h(q)
 \le (1+\delta_n)\int_{\mathcal B_q}h(r)f_S(r)\,dr
 \qquad(q\in\mathcal Q_n,\ |q|<q_0).
 \label{eq:orientation-bin-comparison}$$ Indeed, the integral is at least $\Delta h(q)f_S(q)e^{-C(q_0+\Delta)}$, whereas the lattice mass is at most $(1+e_n)\Delta f_S(q)$ for a deterministic $e_n\to0$. One can take $1+\delta_n=(1+e_n)e^{C(q_0+\Delta)}$. This estimate does not depend on how rapidly $h$ varies. The outermost bin may extend beyond $q_0$, which causes no difficulty because the comparison integral is over the full interval $[-1,1]$.

*Combining the two regions.* Apply <a href="#eq:orientation-bin-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-bin-comparison">[eq:orientation-bin-comparison]</a> with $h=M$. Because the bin interiors are disjoint, their sum is bounded by the full spherical integral. Together with <a href="#eq:orientation-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-tail">[eq:orientation-tail]</a> and <a href="#eq:orientation-spherical-second-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-spherical-second-moment">[eq:orientation-spherical-second-moment]</a>, it yields $$\frac{\mathbb E_O I^2}{S(1)^2}\le1+\delta_n+\tau_n.$$ The first-moment identity therefore gives <a id="eq:orientation-relative-variance"></a>$$\mathbb E_O\!\left[\left(\frac I{S(1)}-1\right)^2\right]
 \le\delta_n+\tau_n\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:orientation-relative-variance}$$ The bin error is independent of the spectrum, and the tail estimate is uniform on $\mathcal G_n$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:cubic-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cubic-deficit">15</a>. Thus the relative variance bound is uniform there. The unconditional assertion follows by Chebyshev’s inequality and $\mathbb P(\mathcal G_n^c)\to0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Small projections and the squared overlap

<a id="stmt-thm-static-small-ball"></a>
<div id="thm:static-small-ball" class="theorem">

**Theorem 17** (Small projections in the Ising law). *Let $W$ be the augmented GOE matrix and let $v$ be a unit top eigenvector. For every positive deterministic sequence $b_n\to0$, <a id="eq:static-small-ball"></a>$$\mu\{x:|v^Tx|\le b_n n^{1/3}\}
 \longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{in probability}.
 \label{eq:static-small-ball}$$ The probability here includes the auxiliary diagonal used to define $v$; the Gibbs measure itself does not depend on that diagonal.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-static-small-ball"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-static-small-ball</summary>

*Proof.* The restricted partition sum has a small first moment, while the unrestricted sum is close to its spherical mean. We choose the auxiliary spectral event after the prescribed threshold $b_n$: take $L_n\to\infty$ with $L_n\le\log n$ and $L_n^2b_n\to0$. For example, for all sufficiently large $n$ one may take $L_n=\min\{\log n,b_n^{-1/4}\}$. In the representation $W=O\Lambda O^T$, take $v=Oe_1$, and define the restricted normalized sum $$I_b=2^{-n}\sum_x
      \mathbf 1_{\{|v^Tx|\le b_n n^{1/3}\}}e^{x^TWx/2}.$$ For every fixed $x$, $O^Tx$ is uniform on the sphere and its first coordinate is $v^Tx$. Consequently Proposition <a href="#prop:spherical-small-ball" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:spherical-small-ball">13</a> gives, conditional on any spectrum in $\mathcal G_n$, <a id="eq:orientation-restricted-first-moment"></a>$$\mathbb E_O\frac{I_b}{S(1)}
 =\sigma_\Lambda\{|U_1|\le b_n n^{1/3}\}
 \le CL_n^2b_n.
 \label{eq:orientation-restricted-first-moment}$$ For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, Markov’s inequality and Proposition <a href="#prop:orientation-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:orientation-concentration">16</a> imply $$\mathbb P_O\!\left(\frac{I_b}{I}>\varepsilon\right)
 \le \mathbb P_O\!\left(\frac{I}{S(1)}<\frac12\right)
       +\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\mathbb E_O\frac{I_b}{S(1)}
 \longrightarrow0$$ uniformly over the good spectra. The ratio $I_b/I$ is the desired Gibbs mass, so adding $\mathbb P(\mathcal G_n^c)$ proves the claim. GOE has a simple spectrum almost surely: the set of matrices with repeated eigenvalues is the zero set of the nonzero discriminant polynomial and has Lebesgue measure zero, whereas GOE has a density. Thus any choice of the unit top eigenvector differs from $Oe_1$ only by sign, which leaves the asserted event unchanged. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-overlap"></a>
<div id="prop:overlap" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:orientation-overlap-tail"></a>
**Proposition 18** (Squared overlap). *Let $Q_n=\mathbb E_{\mu^{\otimes2}}q(x,y)^2$. Then $n^{2/3}Q_n$ is bounded in probability. Equivalently, for every positive deterministic sequence $T_n\to\infty$, $$\mathbb P\{n^{2/3}Q_n>T_n\}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:orientation-overlap-tail}$$ This assertion concerns the original off-diagonal SK disorder.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-overlap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-overlap</summary>

<a id="eq:orientation-weighted-second-moment"></a>
*Proof.* To prove tightness rather than a bound with a fixed logarithmic loss, keep $L_n$ free until the tail threshold has been specified. The same second-moment comparison works with the nonnegative overlap weight $q^2$. Expanding two Gibbs weights gives the exact identity $$\mathbb E_O\!\left[\left(\frac I{S(1)}\right)^2Q_n\right]
 =\frac1{S(1)^2}\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q_n}p_I(q)q^2M(q).
 \label{eq:orientation-weighted-second-moment}$$ The function $q^2M(q)$ is nonnegative, even, and nondecreasing in $|q|$. The central terms therefore satisfy the same bin comparison <a href="#eq:orientation-bin-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-bin-comparison">[eq:orientation-bin-comparison]</a>; the tails are at most $\tau_n$ because $q^2\le1$. Moreover, $$\frac1{S(1)^2}\int_{-1}^1q^2M(q)f_S(q)\,dq
 =\mathbb E_{\sigma_\Lambda^{\otimes2}}
       \left(\frac{U^TV}{n}\right)^2.$$ Here $U,V$ are independent under the tilted spherical law. Lemma <a href="#lem:spherical-covariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spherical-covariance">14</a> bounds this expectation by $Cn^{-2/3}L_n^4$. Thus <a href="#eq:orientation-weighted-second-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-weighted-second-moment">[eq:orientation-weighted-second-moment]</a> gives <a id="eq:orientation-weighted-bound"></a>$$\mathbb E_O\!\left[\left(\frac I{S(1)}\right)^2 n^{2/3}Q_n\right]
 \le CL_n^4+n^{2/3}\tau_n
 \le C'L_n^4
 \label{eq:orientation-weighted-bound}$$ for all large $n$, uniformly on $\mathcal G_n$.

The weighted estimate is now available for every admissible $L_n$. Given an arbitrary deterministic $T_n\to\infty$, choose $L_n=\min\{\log n,T_n^{1/8}\}$ for all sufficiently large $n$. Then $L_n\to\infty$ and $L_n^4/T_n\le T_n^{-1/2}\to0$. Writing $\widehat I=I/S(1)$, conditional Markov’s inequality gives $$\mathbb P_O(n^{2/3}Q_n>T_n)
 \le \mathbb P_O(\widehat I<1/2)
       +\frac4{T_n}\mathbb E_O[\widehat I^{\,2} n^{2/3}Q_n]
 \le o(1)+\frac{CL_n^4}{T_n}.$$ The first error is uniform on $\mathcal G_n$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:orientation-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:orientation-concentration">16</a>. Adding $\mathbb P(\mathcal G_n^c)\to0$ proves <a href="#eq:orientation-overlap-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-overlap-tail">[eq:orientation-overlap-tail]</a>.

The random variable $n^{2/3}Q_n$ has one fixed law for each $n$ throughout this argument: it is a function of the off-diagonal disorder alone. Changing $L_n$ changes only the auxiliary good event and estimates used to prove the assertion, not this law. To verify explicitly that <a href="#eq:orientation-overlap-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-overlap-tail">[eq:orientation-overlap-tail]</a> implies boundedness in probability, suppose tightness failed. Then there would be an $\varepsilon>0$ and increasing indices $n_j$ with $$\mathbb P(n_j^{2/3}Q_{n_j}>j)>\varepsilon.$$ Define a deterministic staircase by $T_n=j$ for $n_j\le n<n_{j+1}$, with an arbitrary positive initial segment. This sequence tends to infinity and contradicts <a href="#eq:orientation-overlap-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-overlap-tail">[eq:orientation-overlap-tail]</a> at $n=n_j$. Conversely, tightness directly implies <a href="#eq:orientation-overlap-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-overlap-tail">[eq:orientation-overlap-tail]</a> by comparing a divergent threshold with any fixed threshold. Finally, because the Gibbs law is invariant under adding a diagonal to $W$, integrating out the auxiliary diagonal preserves the asserted probabilities. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-covariance-scale"></a>
<div id="cor:covariance-scale" class="corollary">

**Corollary 19** (Covariance scale). *For every positive deterministic sequence $M_n\to\infty$, $$\mathbb P\!\left\{
 \frac{n^{2/3}}{M_n}\le\|\Sigma\|\le M_n n^{2/3}
 \right\}\longrightarrow1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-covariance-scale"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-covariance-scale</summary>

*Proof.* The upper and lower bounds use different outputs of the transfer: squared overlap bounds the covariance norm from above, while the top projection bounds it from below. Spin-flip symmetry gives $\mu[x]=0$, so $\Sigma=\mu[xx^T]$ and <a id="eq:orientation-covariance-trace"></a>$$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\Sigma^2
 =\sum_{i,j}\mu[x_ix_j]^2
 =n^2\mathbb E_{\mu^{\otimes2}}q(x,y)^2
 =n^2Q_n.
 \label{eq:orientation-covariance-trace}$$ Since $\Sigma$ is positive semidefinite, $\|\Sigma\|^2\le\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\Sigma^2$. The upper bound follows from Proposition <a href="#prop:overlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:overlap">18</a> with threshold $T_n=M_n^2$.

For the lower bound use Theorem <a href="#thm:static-small-ball" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:static-small-ball">17</a> with $b_n=M_n^{-1/4}$ and put $B_n=\mu\{|v^Tx|\le b_n n^{1/3}\}$. Then $B_n\to0$ in probability and $$\|\Sigma\|\ge v^T\Sigma v
 =\mu[(v^Tx)^2]
 \ge b_n^2 n^{2/3}(1-B_n).$$ On $B_n\le1/2$, for all sufficiently large $n$ this is at least $n^{2/3}/(2\sqrt{M_n})\ge n^{2/3}/M_n$. Both bounds hold simultaneously with probability tending to one by a union bound. The covariance is unchanged by the auxiliary diagonal, so these probabilities are also those of the original SK disorder. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:dynamics"></a>
### Consequences for heat-bath dynamics

The preceding section provides the static inputs used here. A top-eigenvector projection rarely lies near zero, while $Q_n=O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-2/3})$ and the covariance norm has the critical scale. A stationary increment estimate converts the projection bound into a sign test for each realized start. For linear functions, a cavity comparison uses the squared-overlap bound to control the contribution of sites with small average conditional variance, and then compares inverse Rayleigh quotients with the covariance norm. Finally, a density comparison with one fixed subcritical temperature proves the subexponential upper bound and completes Theorem <a href="#thm:mixing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mixing">3</a>.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Stationary increments and typical starting configurations

<a id="stmt-lem-stationary-increments"></a>
<div id="lem:stationary-increments" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:stationary-increments-ct"></a>
<a id="eq:stationary-increments-dt"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Stationary increments). *Fix the disorder and a function $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\mathbb R$. Let $(X_t)_{t\ge0}$ be the continuous-time heat-bath chain with generator $\mathcal L$, and let $(Y_k)_{k\ge0}$ be the discrete-time chain with transition operator $P$, each started from $\mu$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_\mu\bigl[(f(X_t)-f(X_0))^2\bigr]
 &\le 2t\,\mathcal D_\mu(f), \qquad t\ge0,\label{eq:stationary-increments-ct}\\
 \mathbb E_\mu\bigl[(f(Y_k)-f(Y_0))^2\bigr]
 &\le \frac{2k}{n}\,\mathcal D_\mu(f),
 \qquad k\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}.\label{eq:stationary-increments-dt}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the expectations average only over the initial configuration and the trajectory. In particular, if $g(x)=v^Tx$ and $\|v\|=1$, the two right-hand sides are at most $2t$ and $2k/n$, respectively.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-stationary-increments"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-stationary-increments</summary>

*Proof.* For either clock, reversibility diagonalizes the mean-square increment in the same basis as the Dirichlet form. In the inner product $\langle f,h\rangle_\mu=\mu[fh]$, stationarity gives $$\mathbb E_\mu\bigl[(f(X_t)-f(X_0))^2\bigr]
 =2\langle f,(\mathrm{Id}-e^{t\mathcal L})f\rangle_\mu.$$ The operator $-\mathcal L$ is self-adjoint and nonnegative. Expanding in an orthonormal eigenbasis and using $1-e^{-t\lambda}\le t\lambda$ gives <a href="#eq:stationary-increments-ct" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stationary-increments-ct">[eq:stationary-increments-ct]</a>, because $\langle f,-\mathcal Lf\rangle_\mu=\mathcal D_\mu(f)$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:heatbath-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heatbath-form">4</a>.

Each conditional expectation $P_i$ is an orthogonal projection in $L^2(\mu)$. Thus $P=n^{-1}\sum_iP_i$ is self-adjoint with spectrum in $[0,1]$. The discrete stationary increment is $2\langle f,(\mathrm{Id}-P^k)f\rangle_\mu$. For $0\le\theta\le1$, $1-\theta^k\le k(1-\theta)$, while $\langle f,(\mathrm{Id}-P)f\rangle_\mu=\mathcal D_\mu(f)/n$. This proves <a href="#eq:stationary-increments-dt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stationary-increments-dt">[eq:stationary-increments-dt]</a>. Finally, Lemma <a href="#lem:heatbath-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heatbath-form">4</a> gives $$\mathcal D_\mu(v^Tx)
 =\sum_{i=1}^n v_i^2\mu[\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(x_i\mid x_{-i})]
 \le\sum_{i=1}^n v_i^2=1.$$ ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-stationary-increments-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-stationary-increments</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:gibbs-start" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:gibbs-start">1</a>.* We prove the continuous-time statement in three steps. Choose a projection threshold above its root-mean-square increment scale, use its sign as a test for each fixed starting state, and average only the resulting intrinsic bad-state indicator. This last step will remove the auxiliary diagonal without averaging away the distance being tested.

Let $t_n=o(n^{2/3})$ be deterministic and nonnegative. Choose a positive deterministic $b_n\to0$ such that $$\frac{t_n}{b_n^2n^{2/3}}\longrightarrow0;$$ for example, $b_n=(t_n/n^{2/3})^{1/4}+n^{-1}$ works. Put $a_n=b_nn^{1/3}$.

<a id="eq:sign-failure"></a>
*A sign that persists.* Separate the auxiliary randomness by writing $W=A+D$, where $A$ has zero diagonal and $D$ is the independent Gaussian diagonal. The Gibbs law $\mu_A=\mu_W$ and the heat-bath transition laws depend only on $A$. The unit top eigenvector $v=v(A+D)$ is allowed to depend on both matrices. Set $g(x)=v^Tx$. Conditional on $A,D$, start the trajectory from $\mu_A$. If $|g(X_0)|>a_n$, failure of the two endpoint projections to have the same nonzero sign requires $|g(X_{t_n})-g(X_0)|\ge a_n$. Hence Lemma <a href="#lem:stationary-increments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stationary-increments">20</a> and Markov’s inequality give $$\label{eq:sign-failure}
 \mathbb P_{\mu_A}\bigl(g(X_0),g(X_{t_n})
     \text{ do not have the same nonzero sign}\mid A,D\bigr)
 \le \mu_A\{|g|\le a_n\}+\frac{2t_n}{a_n^2}.$$ By Theorem <a href="#thm:static-small-ball" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:static-small-ball">17</a>, the first term tends to zero in probability over $A,D$. It also tends to zero in expectation, since it lies in $[0,1]$. Therefore the deterministic quantity <a id="eq:sign-error-average"></a>$$\label{eq:sign-error-average}
 \varepsilon_n
 :=\mathbb E_{A,D}\mu_A\{|g|\le a_n\}+\frac{2t_n}{a_n^2}
 \longrightarrow0$$ bounds the average sign-failure probability.

<a id="eq:intrinsic-bad-state"></a>
<a id="eq:nested-gibbs-start"></a>
*Testing each realized state.* We now fix the initial configuration before defining its test event. For $x$ with $g(x)\ne0$, let $$H_{v,x}=\{y:g(y)g(x)>0\},\qquad
 r_n(A,D,x)=1-(e^{t_n\mathcal L}\mathbf1_{H_{v,x}})(x).$$ For $g(x)=0$, set $r_n(A,D,x)=1$. This definition includes every zero projection in the failure event, and <a href="#eq:sign-failure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-failure">[eq:sign-failure]</a> implies $$\mathbb E_{A,D}\mu_A[r_n(A,D,\cdot)]\le\varepsilon_n.$$ Spin-flip symmetry gives $\mu_A(H_{v,x})\le1/2$. If $g(x)\ne0$, testing total variation against this half-space yields $$d_{t_n}^{\mathrm{ct}}(A,x)\ge\frac12-r_n(A,D,x).$$ When $g(x)=0$ the following consequence remains valid because $r_n=1$: $$\label{eq:intrinsic-bad-state}
 \mathbf1_{\{d_{t_n}^{\mathrm{ct}}(A,x)\le1/4\}}
 \le4r_n(A,D,x).$$ *Removing the auxiliary diagonal.* The test half-space depends on $D$, but the bad-state indicator on the left does not. We can therefore integrate <a href="#eq:intrinsic-bad-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intrinsic-bad-state">[eq:intrinsic-bad-state]</a> to obtain $$\mathbb E_A\mu_A\{x:d_{t_n}^{\mathrm{ct}}(A,x)\le1/4\}
 =\mathbb E_{A,D}\mu_A\{x:d_{t_n}^{\mathrm{ct}}(A,x)\le1/4\}
 \le4\varepsilon_n.$$ In particular, for every fixed $\delta>0$, $$\label{eq:nested-gibbs-start}
 \mathbb P_A\!\left(
   \mu_A\{x:d_{t_n}^{\mathrm{ct}}(A,x)>1/4\}<1-\delta
 \right)
 \le\frac{4\varepsilon_n}{\delta}\longrightarrow0.$$ This is the asserted convergence in probability over the original off-diagonal disorder. The argument integrates an intrinsic distance; it does not require choosing one auxiliary diagonal that works for a prescribed collection of initial states.

For deterministic integers $k_n=o(n^{5/3})$, use $b_n=(k_n/n^{5/3})^{1/4}+n^{-1}$ and the discrete increment bound. The same proof replaces $2t_n/a_n^2$ by $2k_n/(na_n^2)$ and proves <a href="#eq:nested-gibbs-start" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nested-gibbs-start">[eq:nested-gibbs-start]</a> with $d_{k_n}^{\mathrm{dt}}$. These are distances conditional on the realized initial configuration. Averaging the transition law itself over a Gibbs initial configuration would instead leave it equal to $\mu_A$ by stationarity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Inverse Rayleigh quotients of linear functions

The covariance estimate controls variances of linear functions. To control their inverse Rayleigh quotients, we must also account for sites with small average conditional variance.

We use the standard spin-deletion cavity reweighting; see Talagrand (Talagrand 2011) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Talagrand2011MeanFieldI](#ref-Talagrand2011MeanFieldI)] and Chen (Chen 2013) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Chen2013CavityLocal](#ref-Chen2013CavityLocal)]. Refined cavity and local-field limit theorems have been proved under high-temperature hypotheses by Chatterjee (Chatterjee 2010) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Chatterjee2010Stein](#ref-Chatterjee2010Stein)] and Chen (Chen 2013) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Chen2013CavityLocal](#ref-Chen2013CavityLocal)]. The calculation below uses only exact deletion identities and conditional Gaussian second moments, which hold at the critical temperature.

<a id="stmt-prop-linear-scale"></a>
<div id="prop:linear-scale" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:linear-covariance-comparison"></a>
**Proposition 21** (Linear tests). *With probability tending to one, $$\label{eq:linear-covariance-comparison}
 \|\Sigma\|\le\mathcal R_{\mathrm{lin}}(\mu)
 \le16\|\Sigma\|+n^{13/25}.$$ In particular, $n^{-2/3}\mathcal R_{\mathrm{lin}}(\mu)$ is bounded in probability.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-linear-scale"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-linear-scale</summary>

*Proof.* The denominator in the inverse Rayleigh quotient of a linear function is diagonal in its coefficients. We first rewrite the quotient as a weighted covariance norm, then show that the sites with large weights form a sufficiently small exceptional set.

*Weighted covariance.* For each site define its local field and average conditional variance by $$g_i(x)=\sum_{j\ne i}W_{ij}x_j,
 \qquad \alpha_i=\mu[\mathop{\mathrm{sech}}^2g_i].$$ Thus $0<\alpha_i\le1$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:heatbath-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heatbath-form">4</a> gives $$\mathcal D_\mu(a^Tx)=\sum_i\alpha_i a_i^2,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\mu(a^Tx)=a^T\Sigma a.$$ It follows by the change of variables $b_i=\sqrt{\alpha_i}a_i$ that <a id="eq:weighted-covariance"></a>$$\label{eq:weighted-covariance}
 \mathcal R_{\mathrm{lin}}(\mu)=\|\Gamma\|,
 \qquad
 \Gamma=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(\alpha_i^{-1/2})\Sigma\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(\alpha_i^{-1/2}).$$ For every nonzero $a$, the inequalities $\alpha_i\le1$ and the nonnegativity of the variance give $$\frac{a^T\Sigma a}{\sum_i\alpha_i a_i^2}
 \ge \frac{a^T\Sigma a}{\|a\|^2}.$$ Taking the supremum proves the lower bound in <a href="#eq:linear-covariance-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:linear-covariance-comparison">[eq:linear-covariance-comparison]</a> deterministically.

<a id="eq:cavity-overlap"></a>
*Deleting one spin.* To control a site’s weight, let $\nu_i$ be the Gibbs law on the other $n-1$ spins after deleting site $i$ and all its incident couplings, without rescaling the remaining couplings. Write $\bar\mu_i$ for the corresponding marginal of $\mu$ and set $$Z_i=\nu_i[\cosh g_i],\qquad
 R_i=\mathbb E_{\nu_i\otimes\nu_i}
       \left[\left(\frac{x_{-i}^Ty_{-i}}{n}\right)^2\right].$$ Summing over $x_i$ gives the exact identities $$\frac{d\bar\mu_i}{d\nu_i}=\frac{\cosh g_i}{Z_i},
 \qquad
 \alpha_i=\frac{\nu_i[(\cosh g_i)^{-1}]}{Z_i}
 \ge Z_i^{-2},$$ where the last step is Jensen’s inequality for the reciprocal function. Since $\cosh g_i\ge1$, the product marginal comparison and $(q-x_iy_i/n)^2\le2q^2+2n^{-2}$ imply $$\label{eq:cavity-overlap}
 R_i\le Z_i^2\mathbb E_{\bar\mu_i\otimes\bar\mu_i}
           \left[\left(\frac{x_{-i}^Ty_{-i}}n\right)^2\right]
 \le2Z_i^2(Q_n+n^{-2}).$$

<a id="eq:cavity-max-normalization"></a>
Let $\mathcal F_i$ be the sigma-field generated by the matrix with row and column $i$ deleted. Conditional on $\mathcal F_i$, the law $\nu_i$ is fixed and the incident couplings are independent $N(0,1/n)$ variables. For a fixed spin configuration, $g_i$ is consequently a centered Gaussian with variance $\sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}^2=(n-1)/n$. For every fixed integer $m\ge1$, Jensen’s inequality yields $$\mathbb E[Z_i^m\mid\mathcal F_i]
 \le \mathbb E_{G\sim N(0,\sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}^2)}[\cosh^mG]
 \le2e^{m^2/2}.$$ Taking, for example, $m=200$ and using a union bound gives $$\label{eq:cavity-max-normalization}
 \mathbb P\left(\max_iZ_i>n^{1/100}\right)\longrightarrow0.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:overlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:overlap">18</a> says that $n^{2/3}Q_n$ is bounded in probability. Combining it with <a href="#eq:cavity-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cavity-overlap">[eq:cavity-overlap]</a> and <a href="#eq:cavity-max-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cavity-max-normalization">[eq:cavity-max-normalization]</a>, we obtain <a id="eq:cavity-small-overlaps"></a>$$\label{eq:cavity-small-overlaps}
 \mathbb P(E_n)\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad E_n=\left\{\max_iR_i\le r_n\right\},
 \qquad r_n=n^{-7/12}.$$ For clarity, the exponent slack here is $1/12-1/50=19/300>0$: on $\max_iZ_i\le n^{1/100}$ and $Q_n\le\tfrac14 n^{-2/3+19/300}$, the first term on the right of <a href="#eq:cavity-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cavity-overlap">[eq:cavity-overlap]</a> is at most $r_n/2$, and its second term is at most $r_n/2$ for all sufficiently large $n$.

<a id="eq:cavity-variance"></a>
*Counting exceptional sites.* The uniform polynomial bound on $Z_i$ is not by itself enough. We also need most $Z_i$ to stay below a fixed constant. Conditional on $\mathcal F_i$, for two configurations of the remaining spins put $c=x_{-i}^Ty_{-i}/n$. Their two local fields have variances $\sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}^2$ and covariance $c$, so $$\mathbb E[\cosh g_i(x)\cosh g_i(y)\mid\mathcal F_i]
 =e^{\sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}^2}\cosh c.$$ It follows that <a id="eq:cavity-mean"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[Z_i\mid\mathcal F_i]&=e^{\sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}^2/2},\label{eq:cavity-mean}\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Z_i\mid\mathcal F_i)
 &=e^{\sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}^2}\mathbb E_{\nu_i\otimes\nu_i}[\cosh c-1]
 \le C R_i,\label{eq:cavity-variance}
\end{aligned}$$ because $|c|\le1$ and $\cosh c-1\le Cc^2$ on this interval.

<a id="eq:cavity-exceptional-count"></a>
The event $E_n$ depends on many incident rows. To use <a href="#eq:cavity-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cavity-variance">[eq:cavity-variance]</a> with its stated conditioning, introduce the clipped count $$N_n=\sum_{i=1}^n
       \mathbf1_{\{R_i\le r_n\}}\mathbf1_{\{Z_i>4\}}.$$ The variable $R_i$ is $\mathcal F_i$-measurable, and $4-e^{\sigma_{\mathrm{loc}}^2/2}\ge4-e^{1/2}>0$. Thus conditional Chebyshev gives $$\mathbb EN_n
 =\sum_i\mathbb E\!\left[
    \mathbf1_{\{R_i\le r_n\}}
    \mathbb P(Z_i>4\mid\mathcal F_i)\right]
 \le Cnr_n=Cn^{5/12}.$$ On $E_n$, the clipped count equals $|B|$, where $B=\{i:Z_i>4\}$. Consequently, $$\label{eq:cavity-exceptional-count}
 \mathbb P(|B|>n^{1/2})
 \le\mathbb P(E_n^c)+\mathbb P(N_n>n^{1/2})
 \le\mathbb P(E_n^c)+Cn^{-1/12}\longrightarrow0.$$ No independence between sites, or conditioning on $E_n$ in the Gaussian calculation, is used.

*Combining the covariance blocks.* The probabilistic work is complete. Work on the event $\{\max_iZ_i\le n^{1/100},\ |B|\le n^{1/2}\}$, whose probability tends to one by <a href="#eq:cavity-max-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cavity-max-normalization">[eq:cavity-max-normalization]</a> and <a href="#eq:cavity-exceptional-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cavity-exceptional-count">[eq:cavity-exceptional-count]</a>. Let $G=B^c$. On $G$, $\alpha_i^{-1/2}\le Z_i\le4$, so the principal blocks of the positive semidefinite matrix $\Gamma$ satisfy $$\|\Gamma_{GG}\|\le16\|\Sigma\|,
 \qquad
 \|\Gamma_{BB}\|\le\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\Gamma_{BB}
 =\sum_{i\in B}\alpha_i^{-1}
 \le |B|\max_i Z_i^2\le n^{13/25}.$$ Here $\Sigma_{ii}=1$ by spin-flip symmetry. Positivity also controls the cross block: for $u\in\mathbb R^G$ and $w\in\mathbb R^B$, $$|u^T\Gamma_{GB}w|^2
 \le(u^T\Gamma_{GG}u)(w^T\Gamma_{BB}w).$$ This follows by applying nonnegativity to the quadratic form at $(u,sw)$ for all real $s$. If $a=\|\Gamma_{GG}\|$ and $b=\|\Gamma_{BB}\|$, it follows that $$(u,w)^T\Gamma(u,w)
 \le(\sqrt a\,\|u\|+\sqrt b\,\|w\|)^2
 \le(a+b)(\|u\|^2+\|w\|^2).$$ The same bound holds when either block is empty. Therefore $\|\Gamma\|\le16\|\Sigma\|+n^{13/25}$, proving <a href="#eq:linear-covariance-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:linear-covariance-comparison">[eq:linear-covariance-comparison]</a>. Corollary <a href="#cor:covariance-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:covariance-scale">19</a> and $13/25<2/3$ give the final assertion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-linear-scale-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-linear-scale</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:linear-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:linear-scale">2</a>.* The factor in the linear comparison is fixed, so it can be absorbed while preserving every divergent threshold. Let $M_n\to\infty$ be positive and deterministic. Apply Corollary <a href="#cor:covariance-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:covariance-scale">19</a> with the divergent threshold $M_n/32$, and intersect its event with that of Proposition <a href="#prop:linear-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:linear-scale">21</a>. With probability tending to one, $$\frac{32n^{2/3}}{M_n}\le\|\Sigma\|
 \le\frac{M_n n^{2/3}}{32},
 \qquad
 \|\Sigma\|\le\mathcal R_{\mathrm{lin}}(\mu)
 \le\frac{M_n n^{2/3}}2+n^{13/25}.$$ Since $13/25<2/3$, the final term is at most $M_nn^{2/3}/2$ for all sufficiently large $n$. These inequalities give the claimed common interval for both quantities. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Comparison with the high-temperature model

For the upper bound we combine the high-probability norm estimate of Proposition <a href="#prop:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:spectral">5</a> with the following fixed-temperature, zero-field functional inequality. Its constant may depend on the chosen temperature.

<a id="stmt-thm-high-temperature-gap"></a>
<div id="thm:high-temperature-gap" class="theorem">

**Theorem 22** (High-temperature spectral gap (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[skgapcompanion](#ref-skgapcompanion)]). *Fix $0<\beta<1$. Let $J$ be a symmetric matrix with zero diagonal and independent entries $J_{ij}\sim N(0,\beta^2/n)$ for $i<j$, and let $$\nu_J(x)=Z_J^{-1}\exp\bigl(\tfrac12x^TJx\bigr),
 \qquad x\in\{-1,1\}^n.$$ There is a finite constant $C_\beta$, depending only on $\beta$, such that $$\mathbb P_J\!\left\{
   \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{\nu_J}(f)\le C_\beta\mathcal D_{\nu_J}(f)
   \text{ for every }f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\mathbb R
 \right\}\longrightarrow1.$$ Here $\mathcal D_{\nu_J}$ is the unscaled sum of conditional variances. Consequently, the discrete-time heat-bath chain choosing one uniform site has spectral gap at least $1/(nC_\beta)$ with probability tending to one.*

</div>

The complete proof is supplied by the separate companion (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[skgapcompanion](#ref-skgapcompanion)]. The constant $C_\beta$ is required only for each fixed $\beta<1$: we select $\beta$ after the desired exponential tolerance and before taking the limit in $n$.

<a id="stmt-lem-density-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:density-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:density-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Comparison of conditional-variance forms). *Let $\rho,\nu$ be probability measures with full support on $\{-1,1\}^n$. Suppose $$0<m\le\frac{\rho(x)}{\nu(x)}\le M<\infty
 \qquad\text{for every }x.$$ If $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\nu(f)\le C_\nu\mathcal D_\nu(f)$ for every $f$, then $$\label{eq:density-comparison}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\rho(f)\le \frac{M}{m}C_\nu\mathcal D_\rho(f)
 \qquad\text{for every }f.$$ In particular, for $\rho(x)\propto e^{H(x)}$ and $\nu(x)\propto e^{\beta H(x)}$, with $\beta<1$, one may take $M/m=e^{(1-\beta)\mathop{\mathrm{osc}}H}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-density-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-density-comparison</summary>

<a id="eq:form-infima"></a>
*Proof.* The comparison works directly with least-squares characterizations; one need not compare the conditional kernels pointwise. For any full-support probability measure $\eta$, $$\label{eq:form-infima}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\eta(f)=\inf_{c\in\mathbb R}\eta[(f-c)^2],
 \qquad
 \mathcal D_\eta(f)
 =\sum_i\inf_{h_i}\eta[(f-h_i(x_{-i}))^2],$$ where every $h_i$ ranges independently over all functions of the other coordinates. For the second identity, conditional expectation gives the orthogonal decomposition $$\eta[(f-h_i)^2]
 =\eta[(f-\eta[f\mid x_{-i}])^2]
  +\eta[(\eta[f\mid x_{-i}]-h_i)^2].$$ The first identity follows by the same decomposition with constants. The density bounds and <a href="#eq:form-infima" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:form-infima">[eq:form-infima]</a> imply $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\rho(f)\le M\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\nu(f),
 \qquad
 \mathcal D_\rho(f)\ge m\mathcal D_\nu(f),$$ which prove <a href="#eq:density-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-comparison">[eq:density-comparison]</a>. For the exponential densities, $\rho(x)/\nu(x)$ is a constant times $e^{(1-\beta)H(x)}$; the common normalization cancels in the ratio of its maximum and minimum. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-subexponential-upper"></a>
<div id="prop:subexponential-upper" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Subexponential upper bound). *For every fixed $\epsilon>0$, $$\mathbb P\left\{t_{\mathrm{mix}}\le e^{\epsilon n},\quad
                 k_{\mathrm{mix}}\le e^{\epsilon n}\right\}
 \longrightarrow1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-subexponential-upper"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-subexponential-upper</summary>

<a id="eq:critical-poincare-upper"></a>
*Proof.* We keep the order of parameters explicit: first the requested exponential tolerance, then a fixed subcritical temperature, then the dimension. Fix $\epsilon>0$ and choose $\beta\in(0,1)$ such that $3(1-\beta)<\epsilon$. Let $\mu_\beta(x)\propto e^{\beta H_W(x)}$. The zero-diagonal part of $\beta W$ satisfies exactly the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:high-temperature-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:high-temperature-gap">22</a>; omitting its diagonal changes the Hamiltonian by a constant. With probability tending to one, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mu_\beta}(f)\le C_\beta\mathcal D_{\mu_\beta}(f)
 \qquad\text{for every }f.$$ Intersect this event with $\|W\|\le3$, whose probability tends to one by Proposition <a href="#prop:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:spectral">5</a>. On their intersection, $$\mathop{\mathrm{osc}}H_W\le3n,
 \qquad
 \mu_{\min}:=\min_x\mu(x)
 \ge2^{-n}e^{-\mathop{\mathrm{osc}}H_W}\ge e^{-(3+\log2)n}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:density-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:density-comparison">23</a> now gives $$\label{eq:critical-poincare-upper}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\mu(f)\le C_n\mathcal D_\mu(f),
 \qquad C_n=C_\beta e^{3(1-\beta)n}.$$ We may increase $C_\beta$ to be at least one.

The comparison has produced a full Poincaré inequality. To obtain worst-start total variation for both clocks, combine its spectral decay with the lower bound on the smallest Gibbs mass. We give the conversion explicitly so that the factor $n$ in attempted updates remains visible. By Lemma <a href="#lem:heatbath-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heatbath-form">4</a>, <a href="#eq:critical-poincare-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-poincare-upper">[eq:critical-poincare-upper]</a> gives a continuous-time gap at least $1/C_n$ and a discrete-time gap at least $1/(nC_n)$. Since $P$ is an average of orthogonal projections, its spectrum is nonnegative. Spectral decomposition on the mean-zero subspace therefore yields $$\|e^{t\mathcal L}h\|_{L^2(\mu)}
 \le e^{-t/C_n}\|h\|_{L^2(\mu)},
 \qquad
 \|P^kh\|_{L^2(\mu)}
 \le e^{-k/(nC_n)}\|h\|_{L^2(\mu)}.$$ For a starting configuration $x$, take $h_x(y)=\mathbf1_{\{y=x\}}/\mu(x)-1$, whose squared norm is $\mu(x)^{-1}-1$. Reversibility identifies the transition density relative to $\mu$, minus one, with $e^{t\mathcal L}h_x$ or $P^kh_x$. The $L^1$ expression for total variation and Cauchy–Schwarz give <a id="eq:gap-tv-ct"></a><a id="eq:gap-tv-dt"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \max_x d_t^{\mathrm{ct}}(W,x)
 &\le\tfrac12\mu_{\min}^{-1/2}e^{-t/C_n},\label{eq:gap-tv-ct}\\
 \max_x d_k^{\mathrm{dt}}(W,x)
 &\le\tfrac12\mu_{\min}^{-1/2}e^{-k/(nC_n)}.
 \label{eq:gap-tv-dt}
\end{aligned}$$ With $B_n=\log2+\tfrac12(3+\log2)n$, these bounds imply $$t_{\mathrm{mix}}\le C_nB_n,
 \qquad
 k_{\mathrm{mix}}\le\lceil nC_nB_n\rceil.$$ The two right-hand sides grow at most as a fixed constant depending on $\beta$ times $n^2e^{3(1-\beta)n}$, plus the rounding term. Since $\beta$ is fixed and $3(1-\beta)<\epsilon$, both are at most $e^{\epsilon n}$ for all sufficiently large $n$. The two events used above have an intersection of probability tending to one without any independence assumption. Finally, the mixing times depend only on the off-diagonal disorder, so averaging out the auxiliary diagonal preserves the conclusion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-subexponential-upper-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-subexponential-upper</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:mixing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mixing">3</a>.* The independent upper comparison is now proved. It remains to extract worst-start lower bounds from the stronger realized-start statement. Fix $\epsilon>0$ and apply Theorem <a href="#thm:gibbs-start" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:gibbs-start">1</a> to $$t_n=n^{2/3-\epsilon},\qquad
 k_n=\lfloor n^{5/3-\epsilon}\rfloor.$$ For each clock, with probability tending to one there is a starting configuration whose distance at the displayed time exceeds $1/4$. Worst-start distance is nonincreasing in time by contraction of total variation under a Markov kernel. Hence $t_{\mathrm{mix}}\ge t_n$ and $k_{\mathrm{mix}}>k_n$ on those events. Since $k_{\mathrm{mix}}$ is an integer, the latter implies $k_{\mathrm{mix}}\ge n^{5/3-\epsilon}$, including when $k_n=0$. Intersect these two lower-bound events with the event in Proposition <a href="#prop:subexponential-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:subexponential-upper">24</a> to obtain all four asserted bounds simultaneously. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 25*. Theorem <a href="#thm:linear-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:linear-scale">2</a> retains arbitrary divergent factors because it concerns linear functions. The full Poincaré constant is a supremum over all nonconstant functions, and its upper bound does not follow by enlarging the class in that theorem. Proposition <a href="#prop:subexponential-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:subexponential-upper">24</a> is the independent all-function comparison proved here. The stronger critical exponent results in (OpenAI 2026b, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[criticalmixingcompanion](#ref-criticalmixingcompanion)] use separate arguments and have fixed positive exponent slack in their all-function upper bounds.

</div>

<a id="sec:specification"></a>
### Why an equilibrium model alone has no mixing time

The results in the main text specify both the update rule and the clock. Two elementary constructions show why these are mathematical assumptions, even if the equilibrium law and reversibility are already fixed. A third calculation distinguishes high-probability asymptotic bounds from essential bounds over every disorder realization.

<a id="stmt-prop-arbitrary-refresh"></a>
<div id="prop:arbitrary-refresh" class="proposition">

**Proposition 26** (Arbitrary refresh times). *Fix any realization of the SK measure on $\Omega_n$, $n\ge2$. For every positive integer $r$, there is an irreducible, aperiodic, reversible Markov kernel with stationary law $\mu$ whose worst-state threshold-$1/4$ mixing time equals $r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-arbitrary-refresh"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-arbitrary-refresh</summary>

*Proof.* At each step, refresh the whole configuration from $\mu$ with probability $p$ and otherwise leave it fixed. In operator notation, $\Pi(x,y)=\mu(y)$ and $K_p=(1-p)\mathrm{Id}+p\Pi$, where $0<p\le1$. All entries of $K_p$ are positive, and $\mu(x)K_p(x,y)=\mu(y)K_p(y,x)$. The identity $\Pi^2=\Pi$ gives, for $0<p<1$, $$K_p^k=\Pi+(1-p)^k(\mathrm{Id}-\Pi),\qquad
 \max_x\|K_p^k(x,\cdot)-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 =(1-p)^k(1-\mu_{\min}).$$ Here $\mu_{\min}\le2^{-n}\le1/4$. Choosing $$1-p=\left[\frac{1}{4(1-\mu_{\min})}\right]^{1/r}$$ gives distance exactly $1/4$ at $r$ and strictly larger at every earlier integer. The case $p=1$ also has mixing time one. ◻

</details>

The same freedom remains if transitions are required to change at most one spin. Given the uniform-site heat-bath kernel $P$, the kernel $(1-p)\mathrm{Id}+pP$ has the same stationary law, detailed balance, irreducibility, and transition restriction. Starting from a minimum-mass state, its distance after $k$ steps is at least $(1-p)^k-\mu_{\min}$, by the event that no update was attempted. Its mixing time consequently diverges as $p\downarrow0$. Allowing $p$ to depend on $n$ changes possible growth classes as well.

We next fix the dynamics completely. The remaining distinction is between typical disorder as $n$ grows and all disorder at a fixed dimension. Unbounded Gaussian couplings already separate those notions for two spins.

<a id="stmt-prop-two-spin"></a>
<div id="prop:two-spin" class="proposition">

**Proposition 27** (A two-spin example). *For $n=2$ and the discrete uniform-site heat-bath kernel, the mixing time has no finite deterministic essential upper bound over the Gaussian coupling, although it is finite for every realization.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-two-spin"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-two-spin</summary>

*Proof.* A large positive coupling keeps the two aligned states persistent. This can be quantified using the linear observable $g(x)=x_1+x_2$. Put $w=W_{12}$. Spin-flip symmetry gives $\mu[g]=0$, and conditional expectation computes the eigenvalue exactly: $$Pg=\theta g,\qquad \theta=\frac{1+\tanh w}{2}.$$ Starting at $(1,1)$, the expectation of $g$ after $k$ steps is $2\theta^k$. Since $\|g\|_\infty=2$, the dual total-variation bound yields $$\|P^k((1,1),\cdot)-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ge\tfrac12\theta^k.$$ For each fixed $k$, this exceeds $1/4$ when $w$ is sufficiently large and positive. Every such Gaussian tail has positive probability. The chain for any finite $w$ remains finite, irreducible and aperiodic, so its own mixing time is finite. ◻

</details>

Proposition <a href="#prop:two-spin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:two-spin">27</a> concerns essential bounds over all disorder realizations at fixed $n$. It does not contradict the typical-disorder asymptotic statements in Theorem <a href="#thm:mixing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mixing">3</a>.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The lower and static argument. The direct arrow from the spectral estimates to Haar comparison records their additional use in the cubic overlap deficit. The restricted first moment transfers the small-projection numerator, while partition concentration controls its normalization; a weighted second moment transfers squared overlap. Small projections give the covariance lower bound and squared overlap gives its upper bound. A separate cavity estimate then controls the linear inverse Rayleigh quotients. The independent subexponential upper comparison is outside the diagram.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-structure",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-structure",
          "exact_quote": "The lower and static argument. The direct arrow from the spectral estimates to Haar comparison records their additional use in the cubic overlap deficit. The restricted first moment transfers the small-projection numerator, while partition ",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n box/.style={draw=blue!35!black,rounded corners=2pt,fill=blue!3,\n  text width=7.2cm,align=center,inner sep=5pt,font=\\small},\n branch/.style={box,text width=5cm},\n flow/.style={-{Latex[length=2mm]},semithick,draw=blue!50!black}]\n\\node[box] (spec) {GOE edge and resolvent control\\\\Proposition~\\ref{prop:spectral}};\n\\node[box,below=8mm of spec] (sphere) {Spherical estimates from Gaussian conditioning\\\\Partition, projection, and overlap bounds\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:spherical}};\n\\node[box,below=8mm of sphere] (cube) {Haar first and second moments\\\\Partition concentration and observable transfer\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:orientation}};\n\\node[branch,anchor=north] (small) at ([xshift=-2.8cm,yshift=-12mm]cube.south) {Small-projection mass on the cube\\\\Theorem~\\ref{thm:static-small-ball}};\n\\node[branch,anchor=north] (cov) at ([xshift=2.8cm,yshift=-12mm]cube.south) {Squared-overlap control on the cube\\\\Proposition~\\ref{prop:overlap}};\n\\node[branch,below=8mm of small] (slow) {Sign test for realized initial states\\\\Theorem~\\ref{thm:gibbs-start}};\n\\node[branch,below=8mm of cov] (lin) {Covariance scale and linear\\\\inverse Rayleigh bounds\\\\Theorem~\\ref{thm:linear-scale}};\n\\draw[flow] (spec)--(sphere);\n\\draw[flow] (sphere)--(cube);\n\\draw[flow] (spec.west)--++(-6mm,0)|-(cube.west);\n\\draw[flow] (cube.south)--++(0,-.6)-|(small.north);\n\\draw[flow] (cube.south)--++(0,-.6)-|(cov.north);\n\\draw[flow] (small)--(slow);\n\\draw[flow] (cov)--(lin);\n\\draw[flow] (small.east)--(lin.west);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart diagram with rectangular nodes arranged vertically in five tiers connected by directed arrows. Tier 1 (top): single box containing \"GOE edge and resolvent control / Proposition 3.1\". Tier 2: single box containing \"Spherical estimates from / Gaussian conditioning / Partition, projection, and overlap bounds / Section 4\", connected from Tier 1 by a central downward vertical arrow. Tier 3: single box containing \"Haar first and second moments / Partition concentration / and observable transfer / Section 5\", connected from Tier 2 by a central downward vertical arrow, and directly from Tier 1 via a bypass arrow descending along the left margin and pointing into the left side of Tier 3. Tier 4: two side-by-side boxes; left box containing \"Small-projection / mass on the cube / Theorem 5.3\", right box containing \"Squared-overlap con- / trol on the cube / Proposition 5.4\", both connected by downward arrows branching from Tier 3. Tier 5 (bottom): two side-by-side boxes; left box containing \"Sign test for real- / ized initial states / Theorem 1.1\", connected by a vertical downward arrow from the Tier 4 left box; right box containing \"Covariance scale and linear / inverse Rayleigh bounds / Theorem 1.2\", connected by a vertical downward arrow from the Tier 4 right box."
    }
